It just sort of happens once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control while eating during those times when you have a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what it feels like, though I don’t like saying “out of control.” It sounds so awful.\n\nBut once I start, it’s almost like a switch goes. Part of me is thinking, Linda, stop now, this is ridiculous, but I’m still reaching for more. It’s not quite like normal hunger, if that makes sense. I’m sort of watching myself do it and feeling ashamed, but I don’t stop straightaway.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period happen?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / optician_dispensing / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Since last winter, there has hardly been a week when I haven’t had at least one of those private eating spells and then tried to make up for it by throwing up, skipping meals, or overdoing my walking.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. Since last winter, I suppose… it’s been fairly regular. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.

I know that probably doesn’t sound very reasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe it is different. I mean, other people have said things — my daughter, and a woman from church — that I look too thin. But I don’t really see that when I look at myself.\n\nIf my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale goes up just a pound, I get very anxious, like I’ve somehow become huge overnight. I know that probably sounds exaggerated… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. Not all the time, but there are times when no one’s in the kitchen and I’ll start with, say, leftovers from a church supper, and then crackers and cheese, maybe cookies… and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll have some of that too.\n\nIt ends up being much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a proper meal. I know that probably sounds greedy… I don’t mean it to. It just sort of happens once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control while eating during those times when you have a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what it feels like, though I don’t like saying “out of control.” It sounds so awful.\n\nBut once I start, it’s almost like a switch goes. Part of me is thinking, Linda, stop now, this is ridiculous, but I’m still reaching for more. It’s not quite like normal hunger, if that makes sense. I’m sort of watching myself do it and feeling ashamed, but I don’t stop straightaway.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period happen?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I suppose… it’s been fairly regular. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nUsually it’s private, when no one’s around. And then afterward I try to sort of… make up for it, you know, by being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. I know that probably isn’t the right way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way… I know people mean it kindly. My daughter’s said I look too thin, and a woman at church mentioned it too, so perhaps they’re seeing something I’m not.\n\nBut if my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I get this awful panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight. I know that probably sounds silly… but no, I don’t think I’d feel happier weighing more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I… I’m not sure I’d say I see myself as overweight exactly. That sounds a bit strong, doesn’t it?

I can look in the mirror and know, in a sensible part of my head, that other people might think I’m not big. But then I notice my stomach, or my hips, or how something sits on me, and it feels… wrong. Bigger than it should be, I suppose.

I don’t know if that answers it properly. It’s not that I think everyone else is lying, but I don’t quite see what they seem to see.

Then I’ll go upstairs and make myself throw up, and I sort of cover it by acting like I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed.\n\nI know that doesn’t really answer with a number, sorry… it’s just been happening enough that it stopped feeling like a one-off.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant, anyway. My clothes maybe feel a bit different now and then, but I haven’t been tracking it closely. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very precise answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise more than what's considered typical for someone of your age and health?\n\nPatient: Maybe a bit… I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly athletic or anything. I do have a walking group, and sometimes the next day, if I feel I’ve overdone it with food, I’ll push myself to do extra laps.\n\nAnd, um… sometimes I’ll skip breakfast and lunch the next day too. It’s not really because I feel full of energy. It’s more like I’m trying to make up for what I ate, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds a bit silly at my age, doesn’t it?\n\nEven when my daughter, and a woman from church actually, have said I look too thin, I don’t really believe it. If my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scales are even a pound up, I panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself get enormous overnight. I know that probably doesn’t sound very reasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe it is different. I mean, other people have said things — my daughter, and a woman from church — that I look too thin. But I don’t really see that when I look at myself.\n\nIf my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale goes up just a pound, I get very anxious, like I’ve somehow become huge overnight. I know that probably sounds exaggerated… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. Not all the time, but there are times when no one’s in the kitchen and I’ll start with, say, leftovers from a church supper, and then crackers and cheese, maybe cookies… and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll have some of that too.\n\nIt ends up being much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a proper meal. I know that probably sounds greedy… I don’t mean it to. It just sort of happens once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control while eating during those times when you have a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what it feels like, though I don’t like saying “out of control.” It sounds so awful.\n\nBut once I start, it’s almost like a switch goes. Part of me is thinking, Linda, stop now, this is ridiculous, but I’m still reaching for more. It’s not quite like normal hunger, if that makes sense. I’m sort of watching myself do it and feeling ashamed, but I don’t stop straightaway.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period happen?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I suppose… it’s been fairly regular. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nUsually it’s private, when no one’s around. And then afterward I try to sort of… make up for it, you know, by being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. I know that probably isn’t the right way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way… I know people mean it kindly. My daughter’s said I look too thin, and a woman at church mentioned it too, so perhaps they’re seeing something I’m not.\n\nBut if my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I get this awful panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight. I know that probably sounds silly… but no, I don’t think I’d feel happier weighing more.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as overweight even if others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d say I see myself as overweight exactly. That sounds a bit strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI can look in the mirror and know, in a sensible part of my head, that other people might think I’m not big. But then I notice my stomach, or my hips, or how something sits on me, and it feels… wrong. Bigger than it should be, I suppose.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it properly. It’s not that I think everyone else is lying, but I don’t quite see what they seem to see.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

My daughter’s said I look too thin, and a woman at church mentioned it too, so perhaps they’re seeing something I’m not.\n\nBut if my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I get this awful panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight. I know that probably sounds silly… but no, I don’t think I’d feel happier weighing more.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as overweight even if others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d say I see myself as overweight exactly. That sounds a bit strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI can look in the mirror and know, in a sensible part of my head, that other people might think I’m not big. But then I notice my stomach, or my hips, or how something sits on me, and it feels… wrong. Bigger than it should be, I suppose.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it properly. It’s not that I think everyone else is lying, but I don’t quite see what they seem to see.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t counted exactly, I’m sorry. It’s not every day, but it’s happened enough that it’s what finally made me say something.\n\nUsually if I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then I sort of act as though I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. I don’t make a scene or anything—I usually just pretend I’m tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. But it’s been happening enough that I thought… I probably ought to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: Not every time I eat, no… it’s more when I feel I’ve eaten too much. Then I’ll go upstairs and make myself throw up, and I sort of cover it by acting like I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed.\n\nI know that doesn’t really answer with a number, sorry… it’s just been happening enough that it stopped feeling like a one-off.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant, anyway. My clothes maybe feel a bit different now and then, but I haven’t been tracking it closely. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very precise answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise more than what's considered typical for someone of your age and health?\n\nPatient: Maybe a bit… I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly athletic or anything. I do have a walking group, and sometimes the next day, if I feel I’ve overdone it with food, I’ll push myself to do extra laps.\n\nAnd, um… sometimes I’ll skip breakfast and lunch the next day too. It’s not really because I feel full of energy. It’s more like I’m trying to make up for what I ate, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I suppose I’m here because something’s been worrying me, and I wasn’t sure if I should say it out loud.\n\nSometimes after I’ve eaten too much, I go upstairs and make myself sick. I don’t make a scene or anything—I usually just pretend I’m tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. But it’s been happening enough that I thought… I probably ought to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: Not every time I eat, no… it’s more when I feel I’ve eaten too much. Then I’ll go upstairs and make myself throw up, and I sort of cover it by acting like I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed.\n\nI know that doesn’t really answer with a number, sorry… it’s just been happening enough that it stopped feeling like a one-off.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant, anyway. My clothes maybe feel a bit different now and then, but I haven’t been tracking it closely. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very precise answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise more than what's considered typical for someone of your age and health?\n\nPatient: Maybe a bit… I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly athletic or anything. I do have a walking group, and sometimes the next day, if I feel I’ve overdone it with food, I’ll push myself to do extra laps.\n\nAnd, um… sometimes I’ll skip breakfast and lunch the next day too. It’s not really because I feel full of energy. It’s more like I’m trying to make up for what I ate, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds a bit silly at my age, doesn’t it?\n\nEven when my daughter, and a woman from church actually, have said I look too thin, I don’t really believe it. If my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scales are even a pound up, I panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself get enormous overnight. I know that probably doesn’t sound very reasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe it is different. I mean, other people have said things — my daughter, and a woman from church — that I look too thin. But I don’t really see that when I look at myself.\n\nIf my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale goes up just a pound, I get very anxious, like I’ve somehow become huge overnight. I know that probably sounds exaggerated… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. Not all the time, but there are times when no one’s in the kitchen and I’ll start with, say, leftovers from a church supper, and then crackers and cheese, maybe cookies… and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll have some of that too.\n\nIt ends up being much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a proper meal. I know that probably sounds greedy… I don’t mean it to. It just sort of happens once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control while eating during those times when you have a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what it feels like, though I don’t like saying “out of control.” It sounds so awful.\n\nBut once I start, it’s almost like a switch goes. Part of me is thinking, Linda, stop now, this is ridiculous, but I’m still reaching for more. It’s not quite like normal hunger, if that makes sense. I’m sort of watching myself do it and feeling ashamed, but I don’t stop straightaway.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period happen?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I suppose… it’s been fairly regular. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nUsually it’s private, when no one’s around. And then afterward I try to sort of… make up for it, you know, by being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. I know that probably isn’t the right way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way… I know people mean it kindly. I know that probably sounds silly… but no, I don’t think I’d feel happier weighing more.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as overweight even if others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d say I see myself as overweight exactly. Yes… I think it is, mostly. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds vain, or silly at my age, but it’s not really about wanting to look fashionable or anything.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I suppose I’m here because something’s been worrying me, and I wasn’t sure if I should say it out loud.\n\nSometimes after I’ve eaten too much, I go upstairs and make myself sick. I don’t make a scene or anything—I usually just pretend I’m tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. But it’s been happening enough that I thought… I probably ought to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: Not every time I eat, no… it’s more when I feel I’ve eaten too much. Then I’ll go upstairs and make myself throw up, and I sort of cover it by acting like I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed.\n\nI know that doesn’t really answer with a number, sorry… it’s just been happening enough that it stopped feeling like a one-off.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant, anyway. My clothes maybe feel a bit different now and then, but I haven’t been tracking it closely. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very precise answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise more than what's considered typical for someone of your age and health?\n\nPatient: Maybe a bit… I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly athletic or anything. I do have a walking group, and sometimes the next day, if I feel I’ve overdone it with food, I’ll push myself to do extra laps.\n\nAnd, um… sometimes I’ll skip breakfast and lunch the next day too. It’s not really because I feel full of energy. It’s more like I’m trying to make up for what I ate, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds a bit silly at my age, doesn’t it?\n\nEven when my daughter, and a woman from church actually, have said I look too thin, I don’t really believe it. If my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scales are even a pound up, I panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself get enormous overnight. I know that probably doesn’t sound very reasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe it is different. I mean, other people have said things — my daughter, and a woman from church — that I look too thin. But I don’t really see that when I look at myself.\n\nIf my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale goes up just a pound, I get very anxious, like I’ve somehow become huge overnight. I know that probably sounds exaggerated… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. Not all the time, but there are times when no one’s in the kitchen and I’ll start with, say, leftovers from a church supper, and then crackers and cheese, maybe cookies… and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll have some of that too.\n\nIt ends up being much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a proper meal. I know that probably sounds greedy… I don’t mean it to. It just sort of happens once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control while eating during those times when you have a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what it feels like, though I don’t like saying “out of control.” It sounds so awful.\n\nBut once I start, it’s almost like a switch goes. Part of me is thinking, Linda, stop now, this is ridiculous, but I’m still reaching for more. It’s not quite like normal hunger, if that makes sense. I’m sort of watching myself do it and feeling ashamed, but I don’t stop straightaway.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period happen?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I suppose… it’s been fairly regular. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nUsually it’s private, when no one’s around. And then afterward I try to sort of… make up for it, you know, by being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. I know that probably isn’t the right way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way… I know people mean it kindly. My daughter’s said I look too thin, and a woman at church mentioned it too, so perhaps they’re seeing something I’m not.\n\nBut if my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I get this awful panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight. I know that probably sounds silly… but no, I don’t think I’d feel happier weighing more.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as overweight even if others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d say I see myself as overweight exactly. That sounds a bit strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI can look in the mirror and know, in a sensible part of my head, that other people might think I’m not big. But then I notice my stomach, or my hips, or how something sits on me, and it feels… wrong. Bigger than it should be, I suppose.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it properly. It’s not that I think everyone else is lying, but I don’t quite see what they seem to see.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t counted exactly, I’m sorry. It’s not every day, but it’s happened enough that it’s what finally made me say something.\n\nUsually if I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then I sort of act as though I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. I know that sounds awful. Maybe… several times in a month? I can try to keep track more properly if that would help.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I haven’t kept a proper count by week. It’s usually when no one else is in the kitchen… I’ll start with something like leftovers from a church supper, and then it sort of carries on — crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club.\n\nIt’s much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a normal meal. I know that’s probably the sort of thing you mean… I can try to keep track more carefully if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, since last winter there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI don’t mean every day or anything like that, but usually there’s been one of those private eating spells in a week, and then I’ll try to sort of undo it afterwards — being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I suppose I’m here because something’s been worrying me, and I wasn’t sure if I should say it out loud.\n\nSometimes after I’ve eaten too much, I go upstairs and make myself sick. I don’t make a scene or anything—I usually just pretend I’m tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. But it’s been happening enough that I thought… I probably ought to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: Not every time I eat, no… it’s more when I feel I’ve eaten too much. Then I’ll go upstairs and make myself throw up, and I sort of cover it by acting like I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed.\n\nI know that doesn’t really answer with a number, sorry… it’s just been happening enough that it stopped feeling like a one-off.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant, anyway. My clothes maybe feel a bit different now and then, but I haven’t been tracking it closely. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very precise answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise more than what's considered typical for someone of your age and health?\n\nPatient: Maybe a bit… I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly athletic or anything. I do have a walking group, and sometimes the next day, if I feel I’ve overdone it with food, I’ll push myself to do extra laps.\n\nAnd, um… sometimes I’ll skip breakfast and lunch the next day too. It’s not really because I feel full of energy. It’s more like I’m trying to make up for what I ate, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds a bit silly at my age, doesn’t it?\n\nEven when my daughter, and a woman from church actually, have said I look too thin, I don’t really believe it. If my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scales are even a pound up, I panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself get enormous overnight. I know that probably doesn’t sound very reasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe it is different. I mean, other people have said things — my daughter, and a woman from church — that I look too thin. But I don’t really see that when I look at myself.\n\nIf my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale goes up just a pound, I get very anxious, like I’ve somehow become huge overnight. I know that probably sounds exaggerated… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. Not all the time, but there are times when no one’s in the kitchen and I’ll start with, say, leftovers from a church supper, and then crackers and cheese, maybe cookies… and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll have some of that too.\n\nIt ends up being much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a proper meal. I know that probably sounds greedy… I don’t mean it to. It just sort of happens once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control while eating during those times when you have a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what it feels like, though I don’t like saying “out of control.” It sounds so awful.\n\nBut once I start, it’s almost like a switch goes. Part of me is thinking, Linda, stop now, this is ridiculous, but I’m still reaching for more. It’s not quite like normal hunger, if that makes sense. I’m sort of watching myself do it and feeling ashamed, but I don’t stop straightaway.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period happen?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I suppose… it’s been fairly regular. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nUsually it’s private, when no one’s around. And then afterward I try to sort of… make up for it, you know, by being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. I know that probably isn’t the right way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way… I know people mean it kindly. My daughter’s said I look too thin, and a woman at church mentioned it too, so perhaps they’re seeing something I’m not.\n\nBut if my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I get this awful panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight. I know that probably sounds silly… but no, I don’t think I’d feel happier weighing more.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as overweight even if others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d say I see myself as overweight exactly. That sounds a bit strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI can look in the mirror and know, in a sensible part of my head, that other people might think I’m not big. But then I notice my stomach, or my hips, or how something sits on me, and it feels… wrong. Bigger than it should be, I suppose.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it properly. It’s not that I think everyone else is lying, but I don’t quite see what they seem to see.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t counted exactly, I’m sorry. It’s not every day, but it’s happened enough that it’s what finally made me say something.\n\nUsually if I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then I sort of act as though I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. I know that sounds awful. Maybe… several times in a month? I can try to keep track more properly if that would help.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I haven’t kept a proper count by week. It’s usually when no one else is in the kitchen… I’ll start with something like leftovers from a church supper, and then it sort of carries on — crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club.\n\nIt’s much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a normal meal. I know that’s probably the sort of thing you mean… I can try to keep track more carefully if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, since last winter there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI don’t mean every day or anything like that, but usually there’s been one of those private eating spells in a week, and then I’ll try to sort of undo it afterwards — being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. That’s usually what makes me want to get rid of it or make up for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, no… though it’s probably the strongest one.\n\nWhat finally made me feel I ought to say something is that after I’ve eaten too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. Then I come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen, or getting ready for bed, so no one thinks anything of it. I know that sounds awful. I’m not proud of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Is that the sort of average you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself sick after eating or restrict your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, mostly. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds vain, or silly at my age, but it’s not really about wanting to look fashionable or anything.\n\nEven when my daughter, and a woman at church, have said I look too thin, I still get this horrible panic if my skirt feels tight or if the scale is up even a pound. It’s as if I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight, even though part of me knows that can’t be right. That’s usually what makes me want to get rid of it or make up for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, no… though it’s probably the strongest one.\n\nWhat finally made me feel I ought to say something is that after I’ve eaten too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. Then I come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen, or getting ready for bed, so no one thinks anything of it. I know that sounds awful. I’m not proud of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I suppose I’m here because something’s been worrying me, and I wasn’t sure if I should say it out loud.\n\nSometimes after I’ve eaten too much, I go upstairs and make myself sick. I don’t make a scene or anything—I usually just pretend I’m tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. But it’s been happening enough that I thought… I probably ought to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: How often do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: Not every time I eat, no… it’s more when I feel I’ve eaten too much. Then I’ll go upstairs and make myself throw up, and I sort of cover it by acting like I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed.\n\nI know that doesn’t really answer with a number, sorry… it’s just been happening enough that it stopped feeling like a one-off.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not significant, anyway. My clothes maybe feel a bit different now and then, but I haven’t been tracking it closely. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably not a very precise answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you exercise more than what's considered typical for someone of your age and health?\n\nPatient: Maybe a bit… I wouldn’t say I’m suddenly athletic or anything. I do have a walking group, and sometimes the next day, if I feel I’ve overdone it with food, I’ll push myself to do extra laps.\n\nAnd, um… sometimes I’ll skip breakfast and lunch the next day too. It’s not really because I feel full of energy. It’s more like I’m trying to make up for what I ate, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds a bit silly at my age, doesn’t it?\n\nEven when my daughter, and a woman from church actually, have said I look too thin, I don’t really believe it. If my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scales are even a pound up, I panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself get enormous overnight. I know that probably doesn’t sound very reasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe it is different. I mean, other people have said things — my daughter, and a woman from church — that I look too thin. But I don’t really see that when I look at myself.\n\nIf my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale goes up just a pound, I get very anxious, like I’ve somehow become huge overnight. I know that probably sounds exaggerated… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. Not all the time, but there are times when no one’s in the kitchen and I’ll start with, say, leftovers from a church supper, and then crackers and cheese, maybe cookies… and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll have some of that too.\n\nIt ends up being much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a proper meal. I know that probably sounds greedy… I don’t mean it to. It just sort of happens once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control while eating during those times when you have a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what it feels like, though I don’t like saying “out of control.” It sounds so awful.\n\nBut once I start, it’s almost like a switch goes. Part of me is thinking, Linda, stop now, this is ridiculous, but I’m still reaching for more. It’s not quite like normal hunger, if that makes sense. I’m sort of watching myself do it and feeling ashamed, but I don’t stop straightaway.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period happen?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I suppose… it’s been fairly regular. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nUsually it’s private, when no one’s around. And then afterward I try to sort of… make up for it, you know, by being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. I know that probably isn’t the right way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way… I know people mean it kindly. My daughter’s said I look too thin, and a woman at church mentioned it too, so perhaps they’re seeing something I’m not.\n\nBut if my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I get this awful panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight. I know that probably sounds silly… but no, I don’t think I’d feel happier weighing more.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as overweight even if others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d say I see myself as overweight exactly. That sounds a bit strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI can look in the mirror and know, in a sensible part of my head, that other people might think I’m not big. But then I notice my stomach, or my hips, or how something sits on me, and it feels… wrong. Bigger than it should be, I suppose.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it properly. It’s not that I think everyone else is lying, but I don’t quite see what they seem to see.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t counted exactly, I’m sorry. It’s not every day, but it’s happened enough that it’s what finally made me say something.\n\nUsually if I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then I sort of act as though I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. I know that sounds awful. Maybe… several times in a month? I can try to keep track more properly if that would help.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I haven’t kept a proper count by week. I know that’s probably the sort of thing you mean… I can try to keep track more carefully if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, since last winter there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI don’t mean every day or anything like that, but usually there’s been one of those private eating spells in a week, and then I’ll try to sort of undo it afterwards — being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. I know that probably sounds silly… but no, I don’t think I’d feel happier weighing more.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as overweight even if others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d say I see myself as overweight exactly. That sounds a bit strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI can look in the mirror and know, in a sensible part of my head, that other people might think I’m not big. But then I notice my stomach, or my hips, or how something sits on me, and it feels… wrong. Bigger than it should be, I suppose.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it properly. It’s not that I think everyone else is lying, but I don’t quite see what they seem to see.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t counted exactly, I’m sorry. It’s not every day, but it’s happened enough that it’s what finally made me say something.\n\nUsually if I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then I sort of act as though I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. I know that sounds awful. Maybe… several times in a month? I can try to keep track more properly if that would help.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I haven’t kept a proper count by week. It’s usually when no one else is in the kitchen… I’ll start with something like leftovers from a church supper, and then it sort of carries on — crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club.\n\nIt’s much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a normal meal. I know that’s probably the sort of thing you mean… I can try to keep track more carefully if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, since last winter there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI don’t mean every day or anything like that, but usually there’s been one of those private eating spells in a week, and then I’ll try to sort of undo it afterwards — being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. Is that the sort of average you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself sick after eating or restrict your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, mostly. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds vain, or silly at my age, but it’s not really about wanting to look fashionable or anything.\n\nEven when my daughter, and a woman at church, have said I look too thin, I still get this horrible panic if my skirt feels tight or if the scale is up even a pound. It’s as if I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight, even though part of me knows that can’t be right. That’s usually what makes me want to get rid of it or make up for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, no… though it’s probably the strongest one.\n\nWhat finally made me feel I ought to say something is that after I’ve eaten too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. Then I come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen, or getting ready for bed, so no one thinks anything of it. I know that sounds awful. I’m not proud of it.\n\nDoctor: When you make yourself sick after eating, is it always because you feel you've eaten too much and want to undo that?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… I mean, it’s when I feel I’ve gone too far with eating and I panic a bit, I suppose.\n\nWhat’s been happening is I’ll go upstairs afterward and make myself sick, then come back down and try to act normal — like I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. I don’t like saying it so plainly, but yes, it’s usually about trying to undo what I feel I’ve eaten.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I do have a walking group, and sometimes the next day, if I feel I’ve overdone it with food, I’ll push myself to do extra laps.\n\nAnd, um… sometimes I’ll skip breakfast and lunch the next day too. It’s not really because I feel full of energy. It’s more like I’m trying to make up for what I ate, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I am. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds a bit silly at my age, doesn’t it?\n\nEven when my daughter, and a woman from church actually, have said I look too thin, I don’t really believe it. If my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scales are even a pound up, I panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself get enormous overnight. I know that probably doesn’t sound very reasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe it is different. I mean, other people have said things — my daughter, and a woman from church — that I look too thin. But I don’t really see that when I look at myself.\n\nIf my skirt feels even a bit snug, or the scale goes up just a pound, I get very anxious, like I’ve somehow become huge overnight. I know that probably sounds exaggerated… but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that you would consider excessive in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I think so. Not all the time, but there are times when no one’s in the kitchen and I’ll start with, say, leftovers from a church supper, and then crackers and cheese, maybe cookies… and if I’ve baked something for book club, I’ll have some of that too.\n\nIt ends up being much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a proper meal. I know that probably sounds greedy… I don’t mean it to. It just sort of happens once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control while eating during those times when you have a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what it feels like, though I don’t like saying “out of control.” It sounds so awful.\n\nBut once I start, it’s almost like a switch goes. Part of me is thinking, Linda, stop now, this is ridiculous, but I’m still reaching for more. It’s not quite like normal hunger, if that makes sense. I’m sort of watching myself do it and feeling ashamed, but I don’t stop straightaway.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period happen?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I suppose… it’s been fairly regular. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nUsually it’s private, when no one’s around. And then afterward I try to sort of… make up for it, you know, by being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. I know that probably isn’t the right way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way… I know people mean it kindly. My daughter’s said I look too thin, and a woman at church mentioned it too, so perhaps they’re seeing something I’m not.\n\nBut if my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I get this awful panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight. I know that probably sounds silly… but no, I don’t think I’d feel happier weighing more.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as overweight even if others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d say I see myself as overweight exactly. That sounds a bit strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI can look in the mirror and know, in a sensible part of my head, that other people might think I’m not big. But then I notice my stomach, or my hips, or how something sits on me, and it feels… wrong. Bigger than it should be, I suppose.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it properly. It’s not that I think everyone else is lying, but I don’t quite see what they seem to see.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t counted exactly, I’m sorry. It’s not every day, but it’s happened enough that it’s what finally made me say something.\n\nUsually if I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then I sort of act as though I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. I know that sounds awful. Maybe… several times in a month? I can try to keep track more properly if that would help.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I haven’t kept a proper count by week. It’s usually when no one else is in the kitchen… I’ll start with something like leftovers from a church supper, and then it sort of carries on — crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club.\n\nIt’s much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a normal meal. I know that’s probably the sort of thing you mean… I can try to keep track more carefully if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, since last winter there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI don’t mean every day or anything like that, but usually there’s been one of those private eating spells in a week, and then I’ll try to sort of undo it afterwards — being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. Is that the sort of average you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself sick after eating or restrict your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, mostly. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds vain, or silly at my age, but it’s not really about wanting to look fashionable or anything.\n\nEven when my daughter, and a woman at church, have said I look too thin, I still get this horrible panic if my skirt feels tight or if the scale is up even a pound. It’s as if I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight, even though part of me knows that can’t be right. That’s usually what makes me want to get rid of it or make up for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, no… though it’s probably the strongest one.\n\nWhat finally made me feel I ought to say something is that after I’ve eaten too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. Then I come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen, or getting ready for bed, so no one thinks anything of it. I know that sounds awful. I’m not proud of it.\n\nDoctor: When you make yourself sick after eating, is it always because you feel you've eaten too much and want to undo that?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… I mean, it’s when I feel I’ve gone too far with eating and I panic a bit, I suppose.\n\nWhat’s been happening is I’ll go upstairs afterward and make myself sick, then come back down and try to act normal — like I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. I don’t like saying it so plainly, but yes, it’s usually about trying to undo what I feel I’ve eaten.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake the next day after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try to tell myself I’m just being “sensible” the next day, you know, balancing things out, but really it’s because I’m frightened of what I’ve eaten doing something to my weight.\n\nSo I might skip breakfast and lunch, or if I’m with my walking group I’ll push myself to do extra laps. Not because I’m full of energy or anything… more because I feel I have to make up for it. I know that probably isn’t the healthiest way to put it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s mostly after what I’d call… excessive, yes. I mean, not just a normal plate of dinner, usually.

It just sort of happens once I’ve started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost control while eating during those times when you have a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that is what it feels like, though I don’t like saying “out of control.” It sounds so awful.\n\nBut once I start, it’s almost like a switch goes. Part of me is thinking, Linda, stop now, this is ridiculous, but I’m still reaching for more. It’s not quite like normal hunger, if that makes sense. I’m sort of watching myself do it and feeling ashamed, but I don’t stop straightaway.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short period happen?\n\nPatient: Since last winter, I suppose… it’s been fairly regular. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nUsually it’s private, when no one’s around. And then afterward I try to sort of… make up for it, you know, by being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. I know that probably isn’t the right way to handle it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be happier if you weighed a bit more?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way… I know people mean it kindly. My daughter’s said I look too thin, and a woman at church mentioned it too, so perhaps they’re seeing something I’m not.\n\nBut if my skirt feels a bit tight, or the scale is up even one pound, I get this awful panic, like I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight. I know that probably sounds silly… but no, I don’t think I’d feel happier weighing more.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you see yourself as overweight even if others tell you that you are too thin?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d say I see myself as overweight exactly. That sounds a bit strong, doesn’t it?\n\nI can look in the mirror and know, in a sensible part of my head, that other people might think I’m not big. But then I notice my stomach, or my hips, or how something sits on me, and it feels… wrong. Bigger than it should be, I suppose.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it properly. It’s not that I think everyone else is lying, but I don’t quite see what they seem to see.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month do you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I haven’t counted exactly, I’m sorry. It’s not every day, but it’s happened enough that it’s what finally made me say something.\n\nUsually if I’ve eaten too much, I’ll go upstairs and make myself sick, and then I sort of act as though I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. I know that sounds awful. Maybe… several times in a month? I can try to keep track more properly if that would help.\n\nDoctor: How many times a week do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I haven’t kept a proper count by week. It’s usually when no one else is in the kitchen… I’ll start with something like leftovers from a church supper, and then it sort of carries on — crackers, cheese, cookies, bits of whatever I baked for book club.\n\nIt’s much more than I’d ever put on a plate for myself at a normal meal. I know that’s probably the sort of thing you mean… I can try to keep track more carefully if that would be helpful.\n\nDoctor: On average, how many times a week do you have these episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short period?\n\nPatient: I suppose… if I’m being honest, since last winter there’s hardly been a week when it hasn’t happened at least once.\n\nI don’t mean every day or anything like that, but usually there’s been one of those private eating spells in a week, and then I’ll try to sort of undo it afterwards — being sick, or not eating much the next day, or walking too much. Is that the sort of average you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you make yourself sick after eating or restrict your food intake the next day?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, mostly. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds vain, or silly at my age, but it’s not really about wanting to look fashionable or anything.\n\nEven when my daughter, and a woman at church, have said I look too thin, I still get this horrible panic if my skirt feels tight or if the scale is up even a pound. It’s as if I’ve suddenly let myself become enormous overnight, even though part of me knows that can’t be right. That’s usually what makes me want to get rid of it or make up for it the next day.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you make yourself sick after eating?\n\nPatient: Not the only reason, no… though it’s probably the strongest one.\n\nWhat finally made me feel I ought to say something is that after I’ve eaten too much, I’ve been going upstairs and making myself throw up. Then I come back down and act as if I’m just tidying the kitchen, or getting ready for bed, so no one thinks anything of it. I know that sounds awful. I’m not proud of it.\n\nDoctor: When you make yourself sick after eating, is it always because you feel you've eaten too much and want to undo that?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes… I mean, it’s when I feel I’ve gone too far with eating and I panic a bit, I suppose.\n\nWhat’s been happening is I’ll go upstairs afterward and make myself sick, then come back down and try to act normal — like I’m just tidying the kitchen or getting ready for bed. I don’t like saying it so plainly, but yes, it’s usually about trying to undo what I feel I’ve eaten.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake the next day after one of these episodes of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I try to tell myself I’m just being “sensible” the next day, you know, balancing things out, but really it’s because I’m frightened of what I’ve eaten doing something to my weight.\n\nSo I might skip breakfast and lunch, or if I’m with my walking group I’ll push myself to do extra laps. Not because I’m full of energy or anything… more because I feel I have to make up for it. I know that probably isn’t the healthiest way to put it.\n\nDoctor: Is making yourself sick after eating mainly about trying to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly, I think it is. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds so calculated, and I don’t mean it to be.\n\nIt’s more that after I feel I’ve eaten too much, I get frightened and I go upstairs and make myself sick. Then I come back down and try to make it look normal, like I’m just tidying up or getting ready for bed. So yes… I suppose it is about trying to control what happens to my weight.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a strong urge to purge after eating a normal or slightly large amount of food, or is it mostly after eating an amount that you would consider excessive?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly after what I’d call… excessive, yes. I mean, not just a normal plate of dinner, usually.\n\nIt’s more like if no one’s in the kitchen and there are leftovers from a church supper, or crackers and cheese, cookies, that sort of thing… and I sort of keep going. Or if I’ve baked for book club, I might eat far more of it than I’d ever put on a plate and serve myself as a proper meal. That’s when the urge is strongest, I suppose. I know that sounds awful.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
